# Build log

Deviations from the contract, one paragraph each.

**Family interleave in sampling.** The contract says to shuffle the task product with `random.Random(0)` and cycle. With 192 tasks, the first 4 shuffled tasks were all `counter_to_counter`, which fails the smoke bar (each family must be in the 4-episode run). `arrival.tasks.propose.shuffled` still shuffles with `Random(0)`, then interleaves the two families while keeping the shuffled order within each one. The miss pattern still sits on attempt indexes 9, 19, 29, …

**Sidecar path.** The contract's `embodiment.json` example gives `cartesian_sidecar: "../hero_kitchen.cartesian.jsonl"` relative to `meta/`, but it also places the sidecar at `out/datasets/<room>.cartesian.jsonl`, outside the dataset folder. The writer records `../../hero_kitchen.cartesian.jsonl`, the path that actually resolves from `meta/`. A test checks that it resolves.

**Validator exit code.** The contract's validator section says "Exits 0 when a required file is missing or a slice is wrong", which contradicts the exit codes in 05-repo-map.md. The validator exits 1 in those cases.

**Validation requires a usable video count.** Missing total_videos used to pass structural checks and then crash the CLI while printing its success line. Validation now requires a nonnegative integer, rejecting missing, negative, boolean, fractional, and string counts with INVALID output. Regression tests first accept a valid zero-video dataset, then exercise each malformed count through the CLI.

**`--from-roomplan` objects (superseded, see below).** RoomPlan does not capture small objects, so the RoomPlan bundle has none and no tasks can be proposed from it alone. The first version borrowed the object placements from `scans/<room>/scan.json` when that file existed. The twin is smaller, with only the sink plus a synthesized counter. If the counter inset rejects the scanned pose, the seed falls back to the lowest surviving start pose.

**Timing.** On an Apple Silicon laptop, `demo --episodes 1000 --videos sparse` takes about 1 s for generation and export (1,111 attempts, 111 dropped, 8 mp4s). `--videos all` for 1,000 episodes was not needed and was not timed.

**Real RoomPlan exports.** The first normalizer assumed plain-string categories and a floor at ARKit y = 0. Real exports (2 public ones now in `tests/fixtures/roomplan/`, sources in its README) use keyed enums (`{"chair": {}}`), camelCase (`washerDryer`), a `confidence` enum, a floor around y = -1.5, zero wall thickness, and arbitrary room rotation. `arrival.scan.roomplan` now unwraps enums, snake-cases categories, drops `low` confidence, subtracts the floor height, gives zero-thickness walls 0.1 m, rotates the room so the longest wall runs along +X, moves the wall bounding box to (0, 0), and snaps yaws within 5° of 90° multiples. Tests check that both real exports normalize, compile, rest on the floor, and form closed wall loops.

**RoomPlan duplicate identifiers preserve existing suffixes.** Renaming repeated a identifiers previously claimed a_2 even when another surface already had that identifier, causing a duplicate-id rejection downstream. The renamer reserves all original names and each generated name before choosing a suffix. Regressions cover occupied suffixes, repeated suffixed names, ordinary duplicates, and a complete wall/window bundle whose parent reference must retain a_2.

**Placed objects instead of borrowed ones.** `--from-roomplan` no longer copies objects from the authored scan. `arrival.twin.compile.place_default_objects(twin, seed)` puts a mug and a bowl (catalog sizes) on the counter nearest a sink (synthesized or scanned; dining tables last), rejecting spots over a sink or stove, with `random.Random(seed)`. Objects carry `placed_by: "place_default_objects"`. The proposer now picks the counter that holds objects as the source. Neither public real export contains a kitchen fixture, so they yield 0 tasks; a synthetic kitchen in Apple's encoding (rotated 30°, floor at -1.4) is the test that yields both families.

**Synthesized counter length.** A counter synthesized around a sink or stove is now `max(width + 1.2, 1.8)` m long (was `max(width + 0.4, 1.2)`), so there is room beside the sink to place objects. It inherits the fixture's yaw, with its long axis along the fixture's local X.

**Yaw-aware geometry.** `in_top_rect`, `_rect_distance` and so `on_counter` transform points into the fixture's local frame (`arrival.twin.compile.to_local`). In `layout.yaml`, axis-aligned walls keep their swapped-size convention; oblique walls get `size = [length, thickness, height]` plus `z_rot` from the wall direction.

**`--scans` default.** A relative `--scans` that does not exist in the cwd resolves against the repo root (`arrival.cli.resolve_scans`). A missing room or a missing `roomplan.json` exits with a message naming the path.

**Room summaries refuse colliding output identifiers.** Explicit scan names such as a-b.json and a_b.json normalize to the same room id and previously overwrote each other's twin and dataset artifacts. Summaries now resolve all inputs and reject duplicate output paths, including hero_kitchen versus ./hero_kitchen, before running any room; the CLI reports a usage error with both names. Regression coverage includes punctuation and case collisions and verifies no output is created.

**Upload hardening.** `POST /jobs` streams the body to a temp file in 1 MB chunks, then finds the `scan` part by boundary scanning over an mmap (no `email` parser, no full in-memory copy). A body shorter than `Content-Length`, a missing closing boundary, an empty file, or no filename returns 400. The handler has a 30 s socket timeout. The room page links `out/datasets/<room>_sim` when it exists and shows its `arrival_generator`, `arrival_physically_valid`, `total_episodes` and families (`arrival_families` or `families`).

**WEB: accept reordered multipart disposition parameters.** The upload parser searched for `name="..."` without a parameter boundary, so when `filename` came first it read the suffix of `filename` as the form-field name and rejected a valid scan. The name pattern now requires the start of a parameter, retaining case-insensitive matching and optional whitespace after a semicolon. Two real HTTP regressions put filename before name, including an uppercase parameter without a separating space; both returned HTTP 400 before the fix and now queue the exact original upload bytes. No website markup or styling changed.

**`.env` for Token Factory.** `arrival.models.token_factory.config()` reads `.env` from the cwd or repo root with a small stdlib parser, only when `TOKEN_FACTORY_API_KEY` is unset, and never overrides set variables. Nothing else loads `.env`.

**Scope change: MuJoCo allowed.** The project lead overrode docs/01-decisions.md: MuJoCo is now an optional `sim` extra and the scope includes simulation with drawers, doors and moving people (`arrival/sim/`, built separately). README documents it and lists the remaining gaps.

- Integrator: sim drawer on counter_a was blocked by the closed cabinet door panel overlapping the drawer stack; door width now shrinks to clear the drawer (`door_w`), open_drawer 8/8. Cabinet door hinge axis flipped so it swings outward (it previously rotated into the carcass); open_door was still 0% at this point (IK/reach); MAN-2 below fixes it.

**MAN-1: Panda look and motion.** The arm keeps the Franka kinematic chain and its capsule collision links, now hidden (`group="3"`) under visual-only Panda geometry (`contype="0" conaffinity="0"`): white link housings with dark joint rings, a low link0 base on a pedestal plate, and a Franka-style hand. Contact physics is unchanged. `ik.solve` adds a nullspace pull toward `robot.HOME` and only stops once the posture has settled, so the elbow stays up between waypoints; the worst counter_to_sink IK error dropped from 0.080 m to 0.012 m. `ScriptedExpert` uses whichever of the two equivalent parallel-jaw yaws (yaw or yaw + π) is closer to the hand's heading, so the drawer grasp no longer spins joint 7 by about 3.3 rad. Waypoints play with min-jerk timing, and the six drawer-pull waypoints form one via-point move instead of six stop-and-go pulses. Steps per segment are unchanged, so success and frame counts match the old run: counter_to_sink 8/8 (1408 frames), open_drawer 8/8 (1168 frames). open_door stays 0/4 (MAN-2).

**OPS-1: Upload worker.** `POST /jobs` wrote a queued `status.json` and nothing read it. `arrival.jobs.worker` (`python -m arrival work`) now polls `out/jobs/`, claims the oldest queued job and runs normalize, twin, objects, sim, validate, zip, rewriting `status.json` at each stage with `status`, `stage`, `started_at`, `finished_at`, `error`, `room_id`, `twin_dir`, `dataset_dir`, `zip`, `attempts`, `successes`. The upload is a RoomPlan export when it has `walls` or `objects` entries with `transform`, a `scan.json` when it has `schema_version`; anything else, including non-JSON, fails at `normalize` with the exception text in `status.json` and the traceback in `error.txt`, and the loop continues. The room id is the filename stem in `[a-z0-9_]` plus the job id, so the twin and `<room>_sim` dataset of two uploads never overwrite each other. RoomPlan uploads get `place_default_objects`, as `demo --from-roomplan` does. The web server stays stdlib and still never imports the pipeline; it gained `GET /jobs/<id>/download` (the zip, 404 with the status until `done`) and a jobs table on the index. Measured: a `scan.json` upload with `--episodes 2` ran in 5.9 s, counter_to_sink 1/1 and open_drawer 1/1, 322 frames, zip 77,624 bytes, validator OK; the checked-in `roomplan.json` upload (sink and walls only) also ran 2/2 on the synthesized counter. Not done: no lock between two workers, no retry of failed jobs, no cloud execution (OPS-2).

**SIM-1: Kitchen visuals.** The `hero_kitchen` twin puts the fridge over `counter_a` and the stove over `counter_b` (TWN-6), so the old scene drew counter_b's drawers inside the stove, with their fronts z-fighting the stove's front, and counter_b's top over the burners. Counters now yield to appliances: `_span` trims a counter's carcass and top where an end runs into a fridge or stove footprint, and `drawer_x` treats a stove like a fridge, so counter_b's drawers move from inside the stove to the middle of the counter (the robot base follows). Those are the only physics changes. Everything else is visual-only (`contype="0" conaffinity="0"`; the physical drawer and door panels, handle standoffs, freezer panel and burner boxes are kept but hidden in group 3): recessed fronts with 3 mm reveals and slim steel bar handles on standoffs, a dark backer that closes the open band under the countertop and forms the toe-kick, a 1.5 cm countertop overhang (the fronts move back; the top's edge stays put because the wrist passes about 9 mm from it in open_drawer), a backsplash, a sink rim, drain and faucet, a gas range with a cooktop, four burners, knobs and an oven door, and a fridge with a fixed freezer door and handles on standoffs. counter_to_sink 8/8 (1408 frames) and open_drawer 8/8 (1168 frames) are unchanged.

**MAN-2: Open doors.** Traced in physics, not from the code: the old path asked for a horizontal grasp 55 cm below the shoulder with the base parked inside the door's swing (IK error 18 cm, door joint stayed at 0), and a straight top-down grasp cannot work either because the counter top overhangs to within 2 cm of the bar, so the hand (6 cm thick) lands on the slab. The grasp is now 10° from vertical with the fingers closing along the panel, held 4 cm up the bar, and the base stands 10 cm left of the handle (away from the hinge) and 65 cm out. Segments interpolate in joint space, so the hand first goes out in front of the counter and up, then straight down, then in to the bar; a direct move from HOME swept through the slab. The 75° pull is one via-point move: with stop-and-go segments the commanded arc led the real door by more than the 1 cm finger pad on the wider `counter_a` door (65 cm handle radius) and the bar slid out at 31°. Measured: `counter_b` 70.8°, `counter_a` 72.6°, 8/8 over seeds 0, 3 and 7, with 0 and 2 people. counter_to_sink 8/8 and open_drawer 8/8 unchanged. Not changed: the 0.75 m arm mount, which is why 70° is the ceiling (the far end of the arc is at the arm's reach); the fridge door is not a task. A `link1|robot_base` contact of 1.7 cm exists at rest in every task; it is unrelated and left alone.

**TWN-6: Sample kitchen overlaps.** The fridge, counters and stove now tile the 4 m north wall end to end: fridge 0 to 0.75, `counter_a` 0.75 to 2.2 (1.45 m), stove 2.2 to 2.9, `counter_b` 2.9 to 4.0 (1.1 m). The sink, mug and sponge did not move; the bowl moved from x 0.8 to 1.15 because at 0.8 it sat inside the fridge's footprint (the sim reported a `bowl_1 | fridge_1_box` contact at rest). `docs/04-contracts.md` carries the same numbers. Two tests in `tests/test_roomplan.py` now check that no two hero fixtures overlap (the sink is inset into `counter_a` by design) and that every object rests fully on a counter top and outside every appliance. Fixing the numbers exposed a compiler rule: `_synth_counters` measured the gap from the appliance's centre, so a 0.7 m stove sitting between two counters (centre 0.35 m from each) got a phantom 1.9 m counter laid over both neighbours and the sink, and MuJoCo refused the scene for a duplicated sink geom. An appliance whose footprint is within 0.05 m of a counter (`ADJACENT_GAP`) is now treated as built in; the 0.3 m centre rule is unchanged, so the RoomPlan-format kitchen test (stove 0.25 m from a synthesized sink counter) still gets its own counter. `_span` and `drawer_x` in `arrival/sim/scene.py` keep their appliance handling but are no-ops on the hero room now. Measured on the new layout: counter_to_sink 8/8, open_drawer 8/8, `demo --episodes 1000 --videos sparse` validates, and with the MAN-2 branch applied on top open_door is 8/8 on both doors.

**MAN-5: close drawer, close door, put in drawer, wipe counter.** Four families on top of the four that existed. The close tasks start with the part open (`DRAWER_OPEN` 0.30 m, `DOOR_OPEN` 70°, set in the joint before the settle steps, and a test checks that start state so a pass cannot be a part that never moved) and push the handle bar with closed fingers: straight back for the drawer, along the arc for the door with the same near-vertical hand as open_door. Success is the joint under 0.03 m or 5°. `put_in_drawer` needed three things found in physics rather than planned: objects and drawer trays were in collision groups that ignore each other (the mug fell through the tray), so object geoms now carry `conaffinity="3"` while trays still ignore their carcass; parking beside the open drawer on the mug's side put the elbow into the fridge, so the base parks straight in front with the drawer open only 0.14 m and the front, handle bar and base column accounted for; and the single 49 cm drop from the carry height, interpolated in joint space, bowed back over the counter and the fingers lost the mug on the slab, so the drop is a chain of 10 cm via-point steps with the hand turned parallel to the counter front. `wipe_counter` pinches the sponge across its short side, presses it 4 mm into the top and makes two 30 cm strokes; the sim accumulates the sponge's travel while it is at counter height (`WIPE_MIN` 0.4 m), so carrying it above the counter does not count. Measured: 32/32 over the seven families that run in this room (counter_to_counter is still skipped as unreachable; seed 5, two people), 24/24 on seed 3, counter_to_sink and open_drawer unchanged.

**MAN-4: bowl by the rim.** The bowl was a solid 14 cm cylinder, wider than the 10 cm gripper opening, and was filtered out of the pick tasks. It is now a hollow body: a bottom disc and eight 6 mm wall panels in an octagon, same mass. The expert pinches the rim nearest the robot 2 cm below the lip with one finger inside, the place point carries the same rim offset so the bowl lands centred. 4/4 bowl → sink on the first run; mugs unchanged. Plates, pans and bottles still have no shape in the sim (SIM-4).

**TWN-2: Scan regression suite.** `tests/test_scans.py` parametrizes over every `scans/<room>/` that has a `scan.json` or a `roomplan.json` (both count, so the hero kitchen is two cases) and runs five checks per case: fixture footprints do not overlap (a separating-axis test on the yawed rectangles; a sink or stove inset in a counter, and the counter synthesized around one, are allowed), every fixture bottom is at or above z = 0, every wall endpoint meets another wall's endpoint within 1 cm, `layout.yaml` writes with one row per fixture and wall, and `build_mjcf` loads in MuJoCo. The overlap check xfails only while the authored hero kitchen still has the TWN-6 overlaps `(counter_a, fridge_1)` and `(counter_b, stove_1)`. Those numbers are already fixed on this branch, so that case passes outright. `scripts/render_rooms.py` draws a top-down PNG per room with Pillow (100 px per metre, walls, door/window/opening ticks, floor polygon, fixtures as yawed rectangles with id and type, objects as dots) into `out/renders/`; in `hero_kitchen_top.png` the fridge and stove visibly sit over the counter ends. Suite went from 61 passed to 100 passed, 1 xfailed.

**SCAN: decode reference-photo pixels during intake validation.** Reading an image header and EXIF did not prove its pixel data was readable, so a truncated JPEG could pass the existing-photo check. The validator now decodes pixels inside its existing unreadable-image handler before inspecting EXIF. A regression validates a complete synthetic JPEG, truncates its final bytes and verifies rejection; the truncated file passed before the fix. Existing GPS checks and website UI are unchanged.

**TWN-3: RoomPlan coverage.** Checked against Apple's reference (the `developer.apple.com/tutorials/data/documentation/roomplan/...json` pages behind the docs): `CapturedRoom.Object.Category` has 16 cases (bathtub, bed, chair, dishwasher, fireplace, oven, refrigerator, sink, sofa, stairs, storage, stove, table, television, toilet, washerDryer); `Surface.Category` is floor, door(isOpen:), opening, wall, window; `story: Int` sits on the room, every surface and every object (iOS 17); `polygonCorners: [simd_float3]` is "in local plane coordinates" (iOS 17); a `CapturedStructure` (merged scans) has `rooms: [CapturedRoom]` plus flattened surface and object arrays. The two public exports confirm the JSON spellings `parentIdentifier`, `story`, `polygonCorners` and `{"door": {"isOpen": false}}`, and that the floor transform maps the polygon exactly onto the wall extents (local y runs along ARKit XZ, local z along ARKit Y). Assumed, no real file seen: that a serialized `CapturedStructure` keeps the key `rooms` and that its elements keep their `story`; the flattened form (one dict with per-element `story`) is handled too. The normalizer now emits `doors`, `windows` and `openings` with `wall_id` (parent identifier when it is one of the walls), `size`, `position`, `yaw` and `is_open`, rotated and translated with the room; both real exports put every door and window at 0.00 m from its parent wall's segment. `floors` carries the polygon corners with `z` above the floor; the compiler uses their bounding box for the layout floor, else the wall bounding box as before. One story is kept per bundle, default the one with the most objects, and the floor height is measured on that story alone, so an upper floor's objects land on z = 0. An island is a `counter` or `table` fixture 0.85 to 1.15 m tall more than 0.3 m from every wall; it compiles to `counter` with `island: true`. `storage` is left out on purpose: a freestanding sideboard of that height is still a cabinet, and retyping it to a counter would be wrong. The band is a choice: amal's dining table is 0.822 m tall and 0.77 m from the nearest wall and must stay a dining table. `arrival.catalog.fixtures` maps all 16 categories (the non-kitchen ones to `box`, since RoboCasa has nothing closer) and holds a default size per category, used when `dimensions` are missing or zero. `tests/test_roomplan_coverage.py` builds a 6 by 5 m room in Apple's encoding with the floor at y = -1.4 and checks each category normalizes, compiles to its mapped type, lands on the floor and reaches the layout, plus doors/windows/openings, polygon floors (an L-shape wider than the walls), islands, both multi-story forms, and the two real exports. The two real-export tests in `test_roomplan.py` are unchanged.

**SIM-3: More moving parts.** The stove's oven door, which was a painted-on front, is now a bottom-hinged body with a handle bar and a site (`stove_1_oven_door`), the faucet grew a lever on a hinge (`sink_1_faucet`), each counter run carries wall cabinets with one hinged door per 0.6 m (`counter_a_upper_door0..2`, `counter_b_upper_door0..1`), and two fixture types that the twin already knew but the scene dropped are built: a floor-standing dishwasher with a bottom-hinged door (a counter run whose end overlaps it is trimmed back, the same rule as for a fridge or stove; an appliance wholly inside a run is still not cut out, which matters for a dishwasher beside a sink under a synthesized counter and is left for the twin side) and a counter-top microwave with a side-hinged door. `part_names(twin)` lists every joint with its handle site and an open position, and a test drives each one open, checks the handle moved, lets physics run and checks nothing blew up. Two things came out of measuring instead of guessing. First, wall cabinets at the usual 1.40 m bottom edge broke counter_to_sink outright (0/2): the wrist links reach 1.50 m over the back of the counter while dropping into the sink, so the cabinets start at 1.56 m. Second, a bottom-hinged door balanced on its hinge falls open on its own; the panel now sits 8 mm behind the hinge line so gravity holds it shut, and a test lets the scene rest for 300 steps and asserts every part is still closed. The wall-cabinet run stops 3 cm short of each end so it stays inside the 5 cm wall boxes. A drawer row above the cabinet door bay was left out: its front would sit inside the MAN-2 door panel's collision box and move the handle that MAN-2 was tuned on. Objects now carry `conaffinity="3"` so they rest on drawer trays and open oven doors (the same change MAN-5 made for put_in_drawer). The hero kitchen's rates are unchanged (counter_to_sink 3/3, open_drawer 3/3, open_door 0/2 on main's policy), and with MAN-2 and MAN-5 merged on top every family runs: 24/24 across seven families with two people, 78 tests.

**SIM-4: More objects.** The object catalog (`arrival/catalog/objects.py`) now holds a spec per id: shape, nominal size, mass at that size, friction and colour, for mug, cup, bowl, plate, sponge, apple, bottle (body plus neck), pan (open ring plus handle) and spatula. Mass scales with volume, so a half-height mug weighs half; the mug's visual handle weighs nothing (it quietly added 10 g until a test caught it). `place_default_objects` puts a mug, bowl, plate, cup and apple on a RoomPlan counter, each at a seeded size within 8% of nominal, and skips one that does not fit. `PICKABLE` is the set the expert holds by the body: the cup, apple and bottle go counter to sink 4/4 each with no policy change; the spatula does not leave the counter (0/4 in both orientations) because at 1 cm thick it sits under the grasp height the expert uses, so it stays scenery. The bowl's hollow rim-grasp shape belongs to MAN-4.


**PPL-2 and POL-4: people interactions, a safety metric, and a closed-loop eval.** Walkers used to loop through random free-floor points at least 0.9 m behind the fixtures, so they never came near the arm. Each walker now follows a seeded loop: a free point, an approach point at the back wall, a lane 0.5 to 0.75 m behind the robot base that crosses the strip the arm works in, a 2 to 4 s stop at the counter 0.8 m or more beside the base, and back out. Every other segment is resampled until it stays 0.6 m from the base column (checked over 1,200 seeded loops, 0 violations), and the stop goes on whichever side has the room, which matters for counter_b's drawer base 0.4 m from the east wall. Nobody opens the fridge yet; that needs a person driving the door hinge. `arrival/sim/safety.py` measures, every control step, the horizontal clearance between each robot collision geom (pedestal, links, hand, fingers) and a 0.17 m cylinder around each person; person geoms do not collide in MuJoCo, so contact is clearance reaching zero. The episode dict carries it as `safety`, `episodes.jsonl` as `arrival_min_human_distance_m` and `arrival_human_contact`, and `collect()` drops a contact episode even when the task succeeded (log tag `DROP human_contact`, report field `human_contacts`). `python -m arrival eval` (`arrival/eval/harness.py`) runs N episodes per family through the same env without writing a dataset and reports success rate with a Wilson 95% interval, sim and wall time, human contacts, the smallest clearance and the largest IK residual to `out/eval/<room>_<policy>.json`; `--holdout` draws only the held-out set of 07-eval.md. That set was not held out before: `make_tasks` sampled all four wordings and all four pose offsets, so `sim` datasets contained wording template 4 and the `(0, +0.08)` start. `make_tasks(holdout=False)`, the default, now excludes both; `holdout=True` draws only them. `open_drawer` and `open_door` have three wordings and no start pose, so they keep all three either way. The sim applies half the y offset, so the held-out start is +0.04 m in y. `sim --families all --episodes 8 --people 2` after the split: counter_to_sink 3/3, open_drawer 3/3, open_door 0/2, counter_to_counter skipped, 0 human contacts, the same rates as before. `eval --policy vla` against an unreachable server (`GR00T_POLICY_URL=http://127.0.0.1:9`) used to dump a URLError traceback; it now prints one line naming the URL and the error and exits 2, like the unset case. Measured: eval 4 per family with 2 people, counter_to_sink 4/4 and open_drawer 4/4, smallest clearance 0.233 m and 0.264 m; `sim --episodes 8 --people 2` 8/8 with 0 contacts and clearances 0.23 to 0.43 m; frame counts unchanged (8.80 s and 7.30 s sim time). The first lane placement put the counter stop at the clipped wall position for counter_b and the walk back went through the elbow (clearance -0.22 m, 2 of 4 drawer episodes dropped); the side rule above fixed it. People do not avoid each other, and the eval counts no arm-fixture collisions.

**POL-1 & POL-2: VLM Planner in the loop & dynamic instruction generation.** `arrival.sim.policy.VLMPlanner` is now wired into `collect()`, supporting `--planner vlm` via the CLI. It parses natural-language instructions and room scene inventories using NVIDIA Nemotron on Nebius Token Factory (`role="super"`), with persistent response caching in `arrival/models/nemotron_cache.json` and graceful fallback to the deterministic `rule_plan` when unconfigured or offline. `arrival.tasks.instructions` introduces dynamic instruction generation via Nemotron Lightning (`role="lightning"`), providing diverse wording variations and enforcing a strict disjoint train vs held-out evaluation split (`--instructions nemotron`, `--split train|eval`) as required by the benchmark contract. All unit tests run offline in <10s.

**POL-4: arm-fixture contacts in the eval.** The eval counted contact with people but not the arm hitting furniture or walls. `SafetyMonitor` now also counts, every control step, contacts between an arm link (link1 to link7) and anything that is not the robot, a person or a free object; the hand and fingers are left out because touching handles, drawers and objects is their job. Each episode's summary carries `arm_contact_steps`, `arm_contact_max_depth_m` and the worst pair; the eval report adds `arm_contact_episodes` and the deepest contact per family, and the table gains `arm_hit` and `arm_mm` columns. It is a metric, not a failure: success still comes from the physics check. First result: in the counter_b door tasks link6 touches the east wall (about 1 mm deep, 52 control steps when opening without people), which the human-contact metric could not see. Separately, `eval --families close_drawer` (and `close_door`, `put_in_drawer`) stops with `KeyError: 'joint'` on main because the harness's `_finish_task` only names the joint for the two open families; #27 replaces it with a shared `finish_task()` in collect.py, so this change leaves it alone.

**DEM-3: release QA on a fresh clone.** The README's install, test, `sim` and `validate` steps were run on a fresh clone of main after the nine merges of Oct 17 (Python 3.13, Windows): `pip install -e ".[dev,sim]"` installs cleanly, 145 tests pass in about two minutes, `sim --episodes 8 --people 2 --save-preview` is 8/8 with the contact sheet and overview video written, and the dataset validates. Then every README claim, the REFERENCE commands and the contracts were checked against the code. Stale text fixed: the status date, the suite's runtime (15 s was the pre-sim suite), the `--families all` comment (every family works except `counter_to_counter`), SIM-1 and MAN-1 still marked as top-priority bugs although #2 fixed them, TWN-2 still naming the TWN-6 overlap as an expected failure, REFERENCE saying the VLM planner was not wired in, the contracts' validator line saying it exits 0 on a bad dataset (it exits 1) and the demo sidecar path (the writer puts it in `meta/arrival_cartesian.jsonl`, not beside the dataset). Added: an `arrival/eval/` row in the folder table, a sim-dataset section in the contracts listing how its meta files differ from the demo's, and `docs/RELEASE-QA.md` with the checklist and this run's numbers. The `validate` OK line and three strings in the web page used an em dash, which prints as a broken glyph in a Windows console; they are plain punctuation now.

**TAV-1: Tavily AI Research Agent for appliance specifications & safety protocols.** Built `arrival.models.tavily` and `arrival.tasks.tavily_research.TavilyResearchAgent` to fulfill the hackathon's "Best Use of Tavily" sponsor award ($3,000). The agent automatically queries Tavily Search API for real-world user manuals, handle clearances, hinge kinematics, and safety operating constraints across all fixtures found in digital twins (e.g. dishwashers, gas ranges, refrigerators, microwaves, faucets). `arrival research [--room ROOM] [--query QUERY]` compiles and outputs a structured research report to `out/research/<room>_research.json`. In `sim`, `--tavily` automatically enriches digital twins and passes the retrieved real-world appliance guidelines into the Nemotron `VLMPlanner` reasoning prompt. A persistent response cache (`arrival/models/tavily_cache.json`) and domain fallback knowledge ensure all tests and local generation run network-free and offline. Tested: 6 new unit tests in `tests/test_tavily.py`, 100% offline pass.

**SIM-2: Lighting and materials.** The scene had one ceiling light, one flat fill and a checker floor. It now has a key spot that casts the shadows (warm to cool, placed near the room centre), a cool directional fill from the front and a low warm window light from one side wall, and the headlight only adds a little ambient. Surfaces get materials by colour in `build_mjcf` (`MATERIALS` maps the front, top, backsplash and wall colours to `wood`, `stone`, `panel`, `paint`), so the fixture builders are untouched: the textures are MuJoCo builtins in white, tinted by the existing geom rgba, which keeps the scene a single string with no asset files (a tile floor with grout lines, plank grain on the fronts, speckled stone tops, glossy boards behind the counters). One constraint shaped the backsplash: MuJoCo projects a 2d texture along a box's local z, so a vertical face only varies along x; a grid of wall tiles is not possible with builtin textures, so the backsplash is vertical boards. The task seed now reaches the lights (`build_mjcf(..., seed=task["seed"])`): level within 15%, colour temperature, key position within 0.4 m and the window side all vary per episode, and the three label bands (0.30 to 0.40, 0.60 to 0.81, 0.85 to 1.15 of nominal) cannot overlap. Measured on the overview camera at 640x480 the branch renders about 45% brighter than main at each label, and over 120 seeds the three bands never touch: dim tops out around 60 mean pixel value, neutral runs from the high 80s to about 120, bright from the mid 120s up (the exact integers move by about 10% between GPUs, so the test asserts the ordering and the gap, not the values). Render cost is unchanged (300 steps with two 128 px cameras: 4.2 s before, 3.8 s after) and physics is untouched: `sim --room hero_kitchen --families all --episodes 16 --people 2 --seed 1` is 16/16 over seven families here and on main, the dataset validates, 148 tests.

**MAN-1 follow-up: the wrist camera looks at the gripper, and joint 7 stays off its stop.** Two things turned up when GR00T's loader was run over a sim dataset (PR #38). First, the wrist camera was mounted 8 cm beside the palm looking sideways and 30 deg back toward the wrist, so it never saw the hand or what it held: measured with segmentation rendering over a counter_to_sink, open_drawer and both open_door episodes, the held mug covered 0% of the wrist frame at every step, the hand 0%, and the cabinet fronts, backsplash and fridge 77 to 95%. The camera now sits on a small housing 6 cm beside the palm and looks along the approach axis, tilted 20 deg inward, so the fingertips sit in the lower part of the frame with the straight-ahead view above them. After the change the mug fills 35% of the frame at the grasp and 36% on average while the gripper is closed (27% over the whole episode, approach included), and the drawer and door frames show the handle between the finger pads. The camera name, resolution and the dataset keys are unchanged. Second, holding a fixed world yaw costs joint 7 the whole swing of the base, and with one of the two equivalent parallel-jaw headings that pinned it at its +2.90 rad stop: for 67 of 176 control steps in counter_to_sink (the carry to the sink) and for the last 53 to 62 steps of open_door on both counters (the pull). `ScriptedExpert.reset` now plans both headings (the second is the first rotated a half turn about the approach axis) and keeps the one whose peak |joint 7| is smallest, unless its IK error is worse by more than 5 mm. Joint 7 now peaks at 0.79 rad in counter_to_sink. open_door and close_door plan only their own heading. The flipped one keeps joint 7 at 1.80 rad through the door pull, but with #47's door grasp against the solid panel it opens the door only 26 to 36 deg, so the pull still holds joint 7 on its stop (59 and 46 of 186 steps) and `test_door_pull_keeps_joint7_off_its_stop` is a strict xfail until a door grasp works with the other heading. open_door ends at 73.0 deg on counter_a and 70.9 deg on counter_b, as on main. Planning both costs 0.16 to 0.19 s per episode. A third thing fell out of measuring: on Windows, `collect()` built each episode's `SimEnv` while the previous one was still alive, and when the old renderer was then collected no GL context stayed current, so every frame after the first episode was black (42 of 42 wrist videos in a run, all but the first at mean pixel 0). `SimEnv.close()` frees the renderers and `run_episode` calls it, so the next episode makes its own context current. That run now renders every frame (and takes 136 to 162 s instead of 40 s, because it used to skip the work). Success is unchanged at seed 5 with two people: 42/42 over the seven hero families before and after. 146 tests pass, plus the 2 strict xfails.

**PPL-1: Better humans.** The six-capsule mannequin is now a body built from a seeded height (1.55 to 1.90 m, proportions scaled from it): pelvis, torso, neck, head and hair, upper and lower arms, thighs, shanks and feet, with seeded shirt, trouser, skin and hair colours, so two people in a scene differ and the same seed always gives the same people. Limbs are capsules in the mocap body whose pose is rewritten each control step from a gait phase (distance walked over a 1.3 m cycle): the legs alternate, the arm on each side swings against its leg, the knee bends during the swing, the body bobs 1.2 cm, and the swing fades out over a quarter second when a person reaches a waypoint and stops. Paths no longer assume an empty floor: every fixture footprint is grown by the person's shoulder width and a leg of the loop that would cut through one is routed round it through the grown footprint's corners (shortest path over those corners, resampling a goal that cannot be reached at all), and the free-floor rectangle only counts fixtures on the back-wall half of the room when it sets the work strip, so an island is an obstacle instead of a reason to shrink the floor to nothing. A test adds an island to the hero kitchen, walks three people for 30 s and asserts that no capsule end is ever inside a fixture while at least one person passes close to the island. The hero kitchen's success rates are unchanged, the dataset validates, and the people_xy stream in the dataset keeps its format.


**PPL-2: a person opens the fridge.** The fridge door was already a hinge with a handle site (`fridge_1_door`, 0 to 2.0 rad), but nothing moved it. Now, in 60% of seeds (`P_VISIT`, drawn from a stream of its own so the other seeds walk exactly as before), one walker starts the episode beside the fridge handle, facing the fridge, its own radius plus 0.2 m out from the front: the right hand goes to the bar over 0.3 s, the hinge is driven along a smoothstep from 0 to the seeded angle over 1.2 s, and the person takes a slow step into the doorway on the handle side, stands there 1.5 to 3.5 s, steps back, pushes the door shut the same way and walks their usual loop. The door is kinematic while a person holds it (`qpos` written each control step, `qvel` zeroed), which is how the walkers themselves move, so physics never flings it. Three things came out of measuring rather than guessing. The hand cannot stay on the bar: past about 20° the person is on the inner side of the panel while the bar is on the outer face, so an arm still holding it crossed the panel by 6 cm; the hand now pulls through the first 17° and is fully off the bar by about 30°, the door travels the rest of the way on its profile alone and stops at the planned angle, and on closing the hand pushes the inner face at the free edge down to about 26° and the door shuts alone from 11°; about 45° of the opening swing is unassisted, which reads as momentum in the preview. A recorded episode does not always hold the whole cycle: the 12 settle steps take the reach and the first 0.3 s of the swing, so frame 0 can show the door already 10° open, and a short episode can end with it mid-close. The swinging foot reaches 0.5 m ahead at full stride and the standing foot 0.29 m, so facing the handle put the toes in the door's swing and walking into the doorway put a foot through the open panel; the person now faces the fridge square, the two short steps are taken at 45% speed with the stride scaled to speed, and the planner checks the toes against the panel along the leg, raising the opening angle in 5° steps (a wider swing moves the panel away from the doorway) and declining the visit if no angle works. And the loop's lane runs closer to the base than CLEAR on purpose, so only the legs into and out of the visit are routed round the base. The other walkers route round the swept quarter disc. `SafetyMonitor` takes the door's geoms as person-driven while it is open, so an arm touching it is a human contact and the episode is dropped; the report carries `fridge_visits` and `door_contacts` per family. Measured on the hero kitchen, `sim --families all --episodes 16 --people 2`: seed 5 is 16/16 on #23 and 16/16 here with the same 16 task outcomes, and seeds 3, 5, 7, 11, 13 and 17 give 96/96 with 20 visits, 0 door contacts and 0 human contacts. No visit ever happens on a counter_a task, because the base stands inside the swing plus 0.6 m; counter_b, stove and wipe tasks get them. The door opens to 68 to 79° on the seeds tried, and over 12 s of a visit no person geom but the holding forearm comes within 0 of the door (the forearm touches the bar, within 1 cm), and the visiting person is never inside a fixture. The other walker's swinging foot could enter counter_a at the counter stop on some seeds; that was the same on #23 and is fixed in the next entry. Not done: the person only looks into the fridge; nothing is taken out, and the fridge is still not a robot task.

**PPL-1: feet stay out of the counter at the stop.** The loop's counter stop is 0.35 m from the front, which clears a standing foot (the toe reaches 0.25 to 0.31 m ahead, by height), but a walker arrived there at full stride, where the swinging toe reaches 0.53 to 0.65 m, and then turned square to the counter with the swing still fading: on 16 of 20 seeds a foot went into counter_a, 87 of 12,000 walker steps, the deepest 17.1 cm, measured with the hero base pose and two people over 30 s per seed. The route radius only keeps the body's 0.29 m clear of the footprints, so it never saw the toe. Now the speed into any waypoint with a pause falls with the distance left over the last 0.6 m, to 15% at the end (`BRAKE`); the swing amplitude follows the speed, so a person reaches the stop with the toe 0.29 to 0.36 m ahead. The stop point is 0.35 m from the front, so for the tallest people the brake alone is not enough: what keeps them out is that the slow arrival ends at the arrival radius, 4 to 5 cm before the stop point instead of 1 to 5 cm, which leaves about 3.4 cm in the worst case. The two slow steps of the fridge visit are left at their own 45%, which the visit planner already sized the toes for. After the change the same 20 seeds have no foot in any fixture; the closest any walker geom comes to a footprint is 10.5 cm (the fridge visitor's left forearm) and the closest foot 11.1 cm; 18 other base positions along the counters, seeds 1 to 20 each, also gave no foot in a fixture. The brake adds about a second to the walk into the stop, so a person stands at the counter a little later and a little longer within an episode. `sim --families all --episodes 14 --people 2` at seeds 5 and 3: 14/14 with the same per-family lines and 0 human contacts on both; the minimum human distance changed in 5 of the 28 episodes (the largest change 0.17 to 0.31 m, the closest 0.057 to 0.056 m), because a person now reaches the counter later. The new test walks two people with the robot base set for five seeds and fails if a foot capsule end enters a fixture, and checks that each of the two pauses at the counter (the fridge visit's pauses do not count). Not fixed: a full-stride turn at a waypoint without a pause can still swing a foot into a fixture within about 0.6 m. It never happens in the sample kitchen, where the free floor starts 0.9 m from every fixture, but with the island the clipping test uses, three free-path walkers put a foot 1 to 5 cm into it on 3 of 20 seeds, the same on the code before this change. 152 tests.

**MAN-4: plates by the rim, pans by the handle.** Both were wider than the 10 cm gripper and sat out of the pick pool. The pan already had a handle (SIM-4); the expert now pinches it across its 2.5 cm width at the root, 1.7 cm out from the ring, hand straight down, and the pan goes counter to sink 4/4. It does not travel level: the fingers are position-controlled (kp 800) and squeeze a 2.5 cm bar with about 5 N each, which resists a few tenths of a newton-metre, and a 0.9 kg pan held 15 cm from its centre of mass needs 1.1 Nm, so the pan tips and hangs from its handle for the carry (tilt 45 to 68 deg across four seeds; in one of them the handle rested against the hand body for a few steps), then drops into the basin. Grasping further out on the handle, or closer to the ring so the rim would meet the pad face, made no difference, because the rim top is only 8 mm above the handle. The plate was a solid 12 mm disc with nothing to pinch. It is now a dish built like the bowl (`_ring_geoms` with a separate floor thickness): a 5 mm floor and a rim the pads straddle, pinched at the rim point nearest the robot with the pads just above the floor. Two numbers came out of the probes and shaped it. The closed pads leave a 12 mm gap (the pad boxes sit 6 mm off the finger axis), so a 5 mm rim is only squeezed when the pinch happens to land on an octagon corner, which is what the bowl relies on; the plate rim is 18 mm. And a 2 cm tall plate slips out of the pads at a 35 deg tilt during the lift, while a 3 cm deep plate rides at 16 to 51 deg and lands in the sink 4/4, so the catalog plate is a deep plate (0.22 x 0.03 m, same 0.40 kg). Rim and handle grasps lift through 3, 8, 15 and 22 cm via points so the object settles into the pads instead of being flung as it tips. IK residual is under 1 mm for the plate, up to 4 cm for the pan at the sink (the grasp point is 14 cm off the pan's centre, so the hand goes to the basin's edge), and the fingers touch only the handle or the rim walls while closed. The body-held grasps are unchanged; the bowl goes through the same rim grasp as before and now the same via-point lift as the plate and pan, so its release comes 60 frames (3 s) later and 1 cm higher than on main, still 4/4; `make_tasks` draws pick objects from `GRASPABLE`.

**MAN-5: the arm opens the fridge.** The fridge door has been a hinge with a handle site since SIM-1 (`fridge_1_door`, 0 to 2.0 rad, 4 kg, damping 3), but no task drove it. `open_fridge` is one task per fridge. The base stands 0.1 m past the door's free edge and 0.75 m out from the front, facing it, out of the swing. Success is the hinge past 60°, the same bar as `open_door`. From that base the arm cannot follow the bar through the whole swing: turning the hand with the door folded the elbow (joint 4) onto its stop, and a grid of base positions (0.1 to 0.5 m along, 0.45 to 0.9 m out, four headings) found none that worked. So the motion has two parts. First the fingers close round the vertical bar from beyond the free edge, with the hand turning 30% of the door's turn, and pull the door from 3° to 39°; the bar is in contact on all 40 steps of that pull. Then the closed fingers slide 4 cm off the bar's free side, the hand rounds the free edge 0.9 m from the hinge, and the closed fingertips push the inside face from 39° to 80°. The heading matters: with the first of the two parallel-jaw headings, joint 7 sat on its +2.90 rad stop from the first waypoint, so the hooked pull uses the other one; for the push the fingers are stacked vertically so the hand is narrow along the panel. Which part does what, measured with the arm held still: held on the bar before any pull, the door ends at 2.9°; held where the hooked pull ends, 40.0°; held mid-push at 52° and at 65°, it ends at 55.6° and 68.6°, so the door coasts about 3° once the hand stops and the push carries it the rest of the way. With the gripper left open for the whole episode the hand still pulls the door to 39°, but rounding the edge knocks it back to about 7° and the push never recovers it: the door ends at 9.3°, a failure. Closing the fingers is what lets the hand round the edge without that knock. Every waypoint is in reach (IK miss 0.0 cm on all 21); the tightest is the slide off the bar, where joint 4's target is 0.03 rad (about 1.6°) from its stop; the measured joint never comes closer than 0.04 rad, but the IK clips to the stop silently, so a small change to the base pose could saturate it. Contacts, every control step of one episode: the fingers hold the bar on all 40 steps of the pull, and the palm's collision box also presses the outside of the panel on 24 of them, up to 0.19 cm deep. The slide off the bar is not a clean break: the fingers stay on the bar until the hand is round the edge, pushing the door on from 39° to 41.5°, and rounding the edge then knocks it back to 30.9° over about 0.55 s before it settles at 38.7° for the push. The left finger's drawn box is inside the panel for 2.9 s, from mid-pull at 14° until the hand is round the edge, more than 1 cm for 1.7 s and 1.7 cm at most; the palm's collision box goes 0.76 cm in for three steps during the knock. During the push the drawn fingers stay about 3 cm off the inside face, because the robot model's collision finger boxes reach about 3 cm past the drawn ones, so a rendered frame shows a small gap. Apart from link1 against its own pedestal (a 1.7 cm overlap present in every task on main), no arm link touches anything, nothing touches a wall, and no robot collision geom comes within 5 cm of drawn-only geometry. An earlier version of this plan pulled with one pad to 75° and let the door finish: in fact the pad slipped off the bar at about 40°, the door was flicked, a fingertip drove it the rest of the way by its corner, the finger then rubbed the west wall during the retreat, its drawn box went 2.9 cm into the panel, and the plan missed its targets by up to 4.3 cm. Measured on the hero kitchen: `sim --families open_fridge --episodes 8` at seeds 5 and 7, with no people and with two, 32/32, every door at 79.8°, no human contacts, smallest robot-human clearance 0.25 m. Every episode is the same trajectory: the task varies only lighting and wording, so the seed only moves the walkers. The seven other families are unchanged (`--families all --episodes 7 --people 0 --seed 5`, the same per-task lines as main); with 8 or more episodes `--families all` deals tasks to families in turn, so adding a family changes which task each episode gets compared with main. The hero scene MJCF is byte-identical. 147 tests. Not done: the plan assumes the door is hinged on the fixture's left with the bar on the right, as the scene builds it, and a right-hinged fridge needs the mirror plan. If PPL-2's fridge visit (#73) lands too, nothing in this branch stops a walker driving the same door during an `open_fridge` episode; #73's visit planner declines when the base is within 0.6 m of the swing, which covers this base pose, but the two together are not tested.

**Docs pass.** REFERENCE.md now covers the merged work: the `GET /previews/<file>` route and the redesigned index page, worker requeue of stale `running` jobs and `interrupted:` errors, `research` and `sim --tavily` with `TAVILY_API_KEY`, `GR00T_POLICY_URL`, arm-contact counts in `eval`, the `--res` multiple-of-16 rule, and the iOS app in the known gaps; the duplicated People bullet is gone. The README status rows no longer call the capture app in review or say nobody opens the fridge. 00-START-HERE, 01-decisions and 05-repo-map carry a note that they describe the original MVP contract. Every command in REFERENCE.md was checked against `python -m arrival <cmd> --help`. One mismatch is left for the code: `--families` help in `sim`, `eval` and `work` lists four families, but `SIM_FAMILIES` has nine.

**WEB: ignore Range values that exceed integer conversion limits.** A syntactically numeric preview Range header with thousands of digits caused Python's integer conversion to raise ValueError, returning HTTP 500. The preview handler now treats an unparseable range like an ignored Range request and sends the full existing asset with HTTP 200. Three HTTP regressions cover enormous start, end and suffix values; all returned 500 before the fix. Three additional cases preserve normal partial, suffix and unsatisfiable-range behavior, including body bytes and Content-Range. No website markup or animation changed.

**DEM-4: one-command demo.** `python scripts/demo_all.py --quick` (or `--full`, `--room`, `--serve`) runs scan normalize and compile, the twin layout PNG, a sim dataset on the README's default families, validate, a closed-loop eval of the scripted expert (2 episodes per family) and the contact sheet in one process, with PASS or FAIL and wall time per stage, and writes `out/demo_summary.md` and `.json`. The summary carries the dataset path, episodes, frames, `arrival_physically_valid` (from `info.json`), `joints_are_placeholder` (from `meta/embodiment.json`, which is where it lives), per-family eval successes, demos per hour, the sentence "Scripted expert in simulation, no policy trained." and the cost rows as null. A missing room stops at the first stage with a one-line error and exit 1. `--quick` takes about 25 s and peaks at 0.58 GB RSS; `cli.py` and the Makefile are untouched. Not done: `--full` was not timed.

**INF-1: CI, Makefile and a CPU image.** The repo had no automation. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs on pushes to main and on pull requests (ubuntu-latest, Python 3.11 and 3.12, 20 minute timeout, superseded runs cancelled, read-only token, no secrets and no network calls beyond pip and apt): it installs `.[dev,sim]` with libegl1, libgl1 and libosmesa6, tries MuJoCo offscreen rendering under `MUJOCO_GL=egl` and falls back to osmesa if that fails, runs `pytest -q -p no:cacheprovider`, then `python -m arrival demo --episodes 2` and `validate` on its output. The Makefile has `install`, `test`, `sim` (2 episodes, 1 person, 64 px), `validate`, `demo` (calls `scripts/demo_all.py`, which DEM-4 adds), `serve` and `clean` (removes `out/` only). The Dockerfile is python:3.11-slim with the same GL libraries and `MUJOCO_GL=egl`, installs `.[sim]`, and starts `python -m arrival serve --host 0.0.0.0`; `.dockerignore` leaves out `out/`, `.venv/`, `.git/`, `ios/` and egg-info. `tests/test_infra_files.py` parses the workflow, checks the Makefile targets and `make -n`, and builds and runs the image when a Docker daemon is up. Not verified: the workflow itself has not run on GitHub, and the image was not built on this laptop because the Docker daemon was off (that test skips).

**POL-3: GR00T policy-server contract, mock server and shim.** `VLAClient` sent only `video.ego_view`, but `configs/gr00t/arrival_panda_config.py` and `meta/modality.json` declare two video keys (`ego_view`, `wrist`), so a real GR00T server would have rejected every request. The client now sends both (the wrist frame whenever the observation has that camera) and encodes and decodes through `arrival/policy_server/protocol.py`, which also checks every response: both action keys present, chunks of shape [T,7] and [T,1] with equal T >= 1, finite floats; anything else raises `ProtocolError` (a `ValueError`), while timeouts and HTTP errors stay `OSError`, so the CLI's exit 2 and the eval harness's failure path are unchanged. `arrival/policy_server/mock_server.py` is a stdlib `http.server` with `POST /act` and `GET /health` and three modes: `hold` (the chunk repeats the current state), `noise` (seeded) and `fail_after_n` (HTTP 500 after N requests); `--port 0` binds a free port. `gr00t_shim.py` serves the same contract around Isaac-GR00T's `Gr00tPolicy`, importing `gr00t` lazily and exiting with a message when it is missing. Not verified: the shim has never run against real GR00T (no GPU here), its calls into `gr00t` follow the N1.x API from memory, and whether relative arm actions come back absolute is an assumption. `evaluate(twin, ['open_drawer'], 1, policy_name='vla', people=0, res=64)` against the mock in hold mode completes with `totals.errors == 0`; success is not asserted, since holding still opens nothing. `docs/ref/POL-3.md` has the contract and the GPU command.

**POLICY: use consistent numeric validation in encoders.** Request encoding converted observations directly to floats, silently accepting booleans and leaking raw conversion errors for invalid state values. Response encoding also accepted booleans, although the decoders reject them. Both encoders now use the existing shared numeric validator, preserving shape/finiteness rules and ProtocolError reporting. Six regressions cover boolean, overflow and text values on request/response paths; four reproduced inconsistent behavior and two retain existing response-error behavior. No website changes.

**DAT-2a: offline loader conformance check.** The real GR00T loader has never read our dataset (it needs the GPU box), so `arrival/export/loader_check.py` reproduces what it would trip on, beyond `validate.py`, with no `gr00t` import: parquet dtypes and shapes against `info.features`, `modality.json` slices and `original_key`s, every `task_index` and annotation index in `tasks.jsonl`, timestamps equal to frame_index / fps, episodes at least the 16-step action horizon (with the number of tail-padded windows reported), mp4 frame count, fps and HxWx3 for the first, middle and last episode per camera, per-dimension mean/std/min/max/q01/q99 recomputed from the parquet and compared with `stats.json` and `episodes_stats.jsonl`, and the relative arm action (action[t+k] minus state[t]) being finite, with its range. It refuses any dataset with `arrival_physically_valid` not true or `joints_are_placeholder` not false. `stats.json` stores no q01/q99, so that is a warning and the recomputed quantiles go in the `--json` output. `scripts/check_dataset_loader.py <dir> [--json]` exits 0 or 1. This does not replace a real GR00T load on the GPU box; DAT-1 stays open until that runs.

**EXPORT: report null trajectory vector rows.** Parquet list columns can contain null rows while retaining the expected list<float32> schema. Structural validation called len on those rows and raised TypeError. State/action width checks now flag null rows and the later finiteness check skips invalid vector sequences. Four regressions start from a valid dataset and put a null in the first or last state/action row; the baseline crashed on null rows. Valid vectors and website UI are unchanged.

**EXPORT: resolve the training annotation's task IDs.** Structural validation checked task_index but did not resolve annotation.human.task_description, allowing an unresolved annotation or a different episode's task to pass. Both task-bearing columns now receive the existing tasks.jsonl and episode-task consistency checks, with the affected column named in errors. Two regressions start from a valid two-task dataset and alter only the first episode's training annotation, covering an unknown ID and a known ID belonging to the other episode; both passed unchecked before the fix. No website changes.

**OPS-3a: time and cost report from local runs.** `python -m arrival.eval.cost` (its own `__main__`, no `cli.py` change) reads `out/reports`, `out/eval` and finished `out/jobs/*/status.json` and prints, per room and per family where the file has families, demos per hour, seconds and attempts per successful demo, drop rate and local wall seconds, as a markdown table or with `--json`. Dollars keep the report schema keys. A product row has a number only when `--usage` gives units for it, and with the placeholder rates of `configs/pricing.example.yaml` (marked assumed, not invoiced) it is `measured: false, estimate: true`; `measured: true` needs units from the usage file and a pricing line with `basis: invoiced`. A `teleop_baseline.json` is validated field by field (a bad one raises an error naming the field), and only with a valid baseline for a room that has generated runs does the report print the generated-vs-teleop table, with both sample sizes. Without one it prints "comparison not measured" and no ratio. Nothing was priced or run on Nebius: all 17 tests use synthetic files in tmp_path. Sim reports carry no per-family seconds, so per-family throughput appears for eval files only. Details in `docs/ref/OPS-3a.md`.

**CAP-3a: scan intake tool.** Fifteen teammates will AirDrop a `roomplan.json` and photos, and raw phone JPEGs carry GPS, a device serial and timestamps. `scripts/intake_scan.py <room_id> --roomplan X.json --photos a.jpg b.jpg ...` (module `arrival/scan/intake.py`) creates `scans/<room_id>/` with the export copied byte for byte, `reference_N.jpg` rebuilt from the pixels only (orientation applied, then a fresh image, so no EXIF, ICC or comment survives) at 1600 px or less, a `notes.md` from a template with the eight required fields (location type, phone model, iOS version, lighting, clutter, counter length, counter height, sink width) and `measurements.json`, the machine-readable copy of the three tape values that TWN-1 will read. It refuses a room id outside `[a-z0-9_]`, an existing folder, fewer than two photos without `--no-photos`, a RoomPlan that does not load or has fewer than three walls, and a measurement that is not a number of metres (above 0, at most 20, so centimetres are caught); the folder is built aside and renamed, so a refusal leaves nothing behind. Then it compiles the twin, places the default objects, runs the `tests/test_scans.py` checks by importing that file's helpers (overlap, floor, closed walls), writes the top-down PNG with `render_rooms.render` and prints GO or NO-GO with the fixture list. `validate_scan_dir(path)` returns the list of problems (blank or non-numeric notes fields, fewer than two photos, an EXIF GPS tag, an unloadable RoomPlan). The amal.json fixture is correctly NO-GO, because its two box fixtures overlap the counter footprint. GO is structure and geometry only; metric accuracy is still TWN-1's tape comparison. `tests/test_intake.py` uses synthetic JPEGs with a fake GPS tag and a tmp `--scans`.

**Room summaries read measurements beside explicit scan files.** An imported scan.json outside the configured scans directory previously lost its neighboring measurements.json, or used another room's same-named fallback. The resolved scan's own directory is now checked first, preserving configured-directory and authored-fixture fallbacks. Regressions cover missing and conflicting fallbacks, verify the comparison succeeds, and retain the authored/not-tape flag.

**CAP-1: iOS visual alignment with the website.** The SwiftUI capture app now uses the website's day/night paper, panel, ink and orange colors, a subtle drafting grid, native serif headings and monospaced section labels. The home screen leads with capture, then saved surveys and server setup; scan details and the chrome around RoomPlan share the theme. Named color assets support dark mode, text uses Dynamic Type, and press animation respects Reduce Motion. Scan persistence, recording, upload, sharing and polling implementations are unchanged; no website files changed. Checked: simulator and unsigned generic iOS device builds with Xcode 26.6, rendered home in light/dark and accessibility-extra-large text, and all five iOS bundle/upload tests. Live RoomPlan coaching, save/cancel interactions during a real scan, VoiceOver and RGB-D recording still require supported hardware validation.

**CAP-1: protect saved surveys and complete shares.** Saved-survey deletion now opens a native confirmation naming the survey and explaining that only local files are removed; a full swipe cannot delete a survey directly. Dates use one wrapping text label and job status its own line so saved rows have room at larger text sizes. Sharing is disabled while an RGB-D archive is being prepared or is missing, preventing a share sheet from silently omitting recorded frames. Checked: simulator build, all five iOS bundle/upload tests, and a synthetic survey in Simulator: the accessible Delete action opens the confirmation and Cancel returns to the unchanged saved-survey count. Actual deletion and live LiDAR recording were not exercised in this check. Website files are unchanged.

**EVAL-1a: realism metrics.** `arrival/eval/realism.py` renders the twin from a camera pose (OpenCV intrinsics and `cam_to_room`, offscreen MuJoCo camera, robot and people hidden through a geom group) and scores it against a reference RGB-D frame given as arrays: mean L1 depth in metres over pixels with valid reference depth and a twin hit, RGB mean absolute error and 1 - SSIM (plus PSNR), sample counts, mask coverage, and a count of reference pixels where the twin hit nothing. `evaluate_frames` refuses any frame id outside the held-out list and returns null with a reason for bundles that block the `realism` claim. Measured on the hero kitchen at 64x80: self-render depth 0 m and RGB 0; a 0.15 m shift with a 5 degree turn gives depth 0.18 m, MAE 0.075, 1 - SSIM 0.51. No real frame has been scored; that waits on CAP-2a captures. Details in docs/ref/EVAL-1a.md.

**Realism cameras recover from a parallel Y up direction.** The look-at fallback always chose Y, so a camera looking along Y with a parallel up vector still divided by zero and produced NaN poses. It now chooses X when Y is also parallel, retaining the previous orientation for vertical views. Twelve regression cases cover both view directions and parallel/antiparallel up vectors on all axes, verifying finite, orthonormal, right-handed poses and their position and forward direction.

**REALISM: reject out-of-range confidence-map values.** Local realism evaluation treated any confidence value above its threshold as trusted, so a grayscale map filled with 255 became high-confidence evidence despite the ARKit 0/1/2 contract. It now rejects values above 2 before applying the existing threshold, consistent with upload validation. Synthetic-bundle tests cover rejection of 255 and unchanged masks for levels 0, 1 and 2; the invalid map was accepted before the fix. No website changes.

**DAT-4a: seed-demo replay retargeting (first slice of #62).** `arrival/generate/retarget.py` records one scripted-expert episode as its eef waypoints, each relative to an anchor (approach, grasp and lift to the object body position; carry, place, release and retreat to the place point), then replays it for new object start poses and place-point offsets. A `ReplayPolicy` (a `ScriptedExpert` subclass whose only change is where the waypoints come from) re-anchors every waypoint, solves IK with `arrival/sim/ik.py` and plays the same min-jerk targets through `SimEnv`. The same physics oracle judges each replay: a plan whose worst IK error exceeds 2 cm is dropped before any rollout (out of reach), a failed oracle check is dropped after it, and neither is written. Successes go through `SimDatasetWriter`, one episode in memory at a time, then `info.json` is rewritten with `arrival_generator = "seed_replay_retarget"`, `arrival_physically_valid = true` (because the oracle checked each one), `arrival_seed_episode_id`, `arrival_seed_ref` (the seed waypoints in `seeds/<id>.json`) and attempts, successes and drops. The label is deliberately not `mimicgen` or `scripted_retarget`: this is object-centric segment replay in the spirit of MimicGen, translation only (no object yaw), pick-and-place families only, one seed. Measured on hero_kitchen, `counter_to_sink`, `--people 0`, res 64, 6 attempts: 6/6 exported, object start xy std 5.8 cm (x) and 3.6 cm (y); 22.1 s (979 demos/hour) against 18.9 s (1146 demos/hour) for the scripted expert on 6 tasks, so replay is not faster here (it plans IK twice per attempt to test reach, and it adds the seed run); its value is varying the start from one recorded demo. Peak RSS 390 MB. `tests/test_retarget.py` (3 offline tests) covers a labelled dataset that passes `validate`, differing start poses, and an out-of-reach start (1.2 m off the counter) being dropped. Details in docs/ref/DAT-4a.md.

**CAP-2: preserve frame archives when ZIP creation fails.** `ZipWriter` previously removed an existing archive and wrote directly to its final path. A read failure after the first entry left a partial `frames.zip`, which later operations could mistake for a finished archive. ZIP creation now writes to a unique sibling temporary file, closes the complete archive, then replaces or moves it into place. Deferred cleanup removes staging files on failure without touching the previous destination. Three offline Swift-driver regressions cover failure with/without an existing archive and successful replacement checked with Python's ZIP reader; both failure cases reproduce the old bug. The test fixture removes read permission from one synthetic file and restores it in `finally`; it skips only if the host does not enforce that permission. Website files are unchanged. Checked on the standalone archive branch: all eight iOS bundle/archive tests, the simulator build, lint, documentation links and diff whitespace checks pass. Read-only review found no actionable issues. Disk-full and process-termination scenarios were not exercised.

**CAP-2a: RGB-D scan-frame format.** `arrival/scan/rgbd.py` defines `frames_manifest.json` (ARKit camera axes x right, y up, z backward; room frame Z-up metres; depth as float32 metres `.npy` or 16-bit millimetre PNG; per-frame intrinsics, camera-to-room and a train/heldout split), a strict `load_manifest` that rejects a wrong unit, non-monotonic timestamps, a non-orthonormal or non-finite transform, a missing file and a frame in both splits, each under its own error code, plus `unproject`, `project` and a synthetic two-camera bundle (floor and wall). In tests a known pixel unprojects within 1e-6 and round-trips, and both cameras land on the floor plane within 1 mm. Only the format exists: the iOS recorder is blocked on a device and nothing consumes the bundles yet.

**Export rejects undeclared episode cameras.** The writer refuses video keys absent from its configured camera list before writing parquet or MP4 files or changing episode counters. Regression coverage checks all, sparse, and none policies, preserves writer state after rejection, and accepts a subsequent valid episode.

**CAP-2: reject nonpositive focal lengths before recording frames.** The Swift bundle writer checked that intrinsics were finite but accepted zero or negative fx/fy, although the server's RGB-D loader requires both to be positive. One such frame could make the entire exported bundle fail validation. The existing skip-before-writing guard now also requires positive focal lengths. Four regressions insert one invalid frame ahead of six valid frames, compile and run the actual Swift writer, check that only six are kept and load the resulting manifest with the server's RGB-D loader. All four cases kept seven frames before the fix. No changes to valid calibration values, capture timing or website.

**DEM-3a: README claim checker.** `python scripts/check_docs.py` parses README.md, docs/REFERENCE.md and ios/README.md and prints one line per problem (exit 1, else 0): every fenced `python -m arrival ...` command is run through the parser `arrival.cli.build_parser()` returns (nothing executes), relative links and backticked `arrival/`, `scripts/`, `tests/`, `docs/`, `configs/` paths must exist, `tests/test_x.py::name` must name a real test, a status line may not list one task ID as both Done and Open, and a sentence tying `arrival_physically_valid` or `joints_are_placeholder` to `python -m arrival demo` or `sim` must match the constant the writer sets. `tests/test_docs_claims.py` runs it on the repo and on a temp copy with an invented flag, a broken link and a missing test reference. The first run found one false claim: the TWN-1 line named `tests/test_lidar_accuracy.py` as if it existed; it is now marked as planned. The `pyproject.toml` description no longer says "MVP, placeholder motion". The untracked `walkin_arrival.egg-info` (and `arrival_sim.egg-info`) in the checkout is a stale editable-install leftover; it is git-ignored and was left alone.

**TWN-1a: scan accuracy harness.** `arrival.twin.accuracy` compares a compiled twin with `scans/<room>/measurements.json` (counter length, counter height, sink width, optional island, stove, fridge and dishwasher widths) and returns the signed error per dimension and a pass/fail at `tolerance_m` (default 5 cm); `scripts/scan_accuracy.py` prints the table for every measured room and exits 1 when any is over. The counter length is the longest contiguous run of scanned counters, with a built-in stove or dishwasher bridging two counters (the tape goes across the cooktop); the legs of an L or U never join, so the longest leg is compared, which the rule in `docs/ref/TWN-1a.md` spells out. Islands, dining tables and synthesized counters are not measured. No real room has been tape-measured yet, so the real-room test skips; the hero kitchen is authored, and its `measurements.json` is a fixture under `tests/fixtures/measurements/` labelled `authored, not tape-measured`, which proves the extraction matches `scan.json` to 1 mm but says nothing about scan accuracy. A 6 cm deviation fails and 4 cm passes in the tests. TWN-1 stays open until CAP-3 produces measurements.

**SIM-7a: appearance variation, offline part.** `arrival/render/variation.py` restyles about 10% of the frames of a finished sim dataset into a COPY directory; the source is never written (the test hashes the tree before and after and runs `arrival validate` on both). Frames are chosen per episode as the round(0.1 x length) frames with the smallest sha256 of (episode, frame, seed), so the share is exact and the pick is reproducible. Two backends: `cosmos`, an HTTP client for `COSMOS_URL` (PNG in, image out, timeout, retries, per-frame latency, the rest of the run skipped after 3 failures in a row), and `local_stub`, a seeded colour, brightness, contrast, hue and 3x3 blur jitter that never moves geometry and is labelled `local_stub`. A restyled frame is accepted only if its Sobel edge map correlates at 0.6 or more with the original and, when a robot/object mask is given, the masked region (grey values normalised inside the mask, so brightness alone does not count) changed less than 0.35; otherwise the status is `rejected` and the frame stays original. Output: `meta/arrival_variation.jsonl` (original id, variant id, backend, model, version, parameters, latency_ms, status, reason), `meta/arrival_variation_summary.json` and `variation_videos/` in the copy. `arrival_appearance_variation` is `cosmos` only when at least one Cosmos request came back and passed the checks, `local_stub` when only the stub produced frames, else `none`. Added latency and USD are `null`. The Cosmos client was only run against a local mock http server: no real Cosmos endpoint was called, so its request format is an assumption until SIM-7 meets one. Variant mp4s are re-encoded whole episodes, so unselected frames in them carry h264 noise. 7 tests.

**G2 (offline slice): golden wire format for the policy server.** `tests/golden/policy_wire.json` holds, for each of two schemas, one request and one response. Every request has both cameras (`video.ego_view` and `video.wrist`, 2x2 PNGs), `state.single_arm` (7 floats), `state.gripper` (1 float) and the task description. The `arm` schema is 8 floats per state and per action step; the `mobile` schema is 11 (the same 8 plus the base x, y, yaw, sent as `state.base` and returned as `action.base`, a [3,3] block in the golden chunk). The file was generated from `protocol.py` itself, so it pins the format against accidental change and is not a capture from a real server. `tests/test_policy_wire_golden.py` checks the encoder against it (pixels compared, not PNG bytes), decodes both chunks ([3,8] and [3,11]), maps the base key through the shim, and checks the errors. Mismatch found and fixed: since MAN-3 the sim state is always 11 wide, but the wire only knew 8, and `encode_request` cut any longer state to its first 8 without saying so (a mobile run would have lost its base pose and its base actions). `encode_request` now takes a schema (`VLAClient` picks `mobile` when the env has a mobile base, or from its `schema` argument), the arm schema drops the base on purpose, and an unknown width, a schema/response mismatch, a bad base shape or a frame that is not HxWx3 raises `ProtocolError` that names the widths. `to_observation` and `to_chunk` in the shim carry a `base` key. Not done: nothing was run against Isaac-GR00T, the shim's calls into `gr00t` are still written from memory of its API and unchecked, and the mobile modality config's `base` key was not tried on a real server.

**Fixtures on any wall (#42).** The sim placed the base by adding the fixture's front normal to y only, and took the walkers' floor as "y beyond the deepest fixture plus 0.9 m", so a counter facing +x or -x parked the robot inside its own carcass (seed 6, no people, rotated kitchen: 5 of 14 episodes, `counter_to_sink` 0/2, `open_drawer` 1/2, base clearance 0.0 m on every west and east task) and any run off the north wall inverted the floor rectangle and crashed `Walkers` with `ValueError: high - low < 0` before the first step. `SimEnv._park` now puts the base `along` the run and `out` from the front face in the fixture's own frame, for every family. `_free_floor` works in the frame where the task's fixture faces +y: the strip starts 0.9 m past the deepest fixture facing the same way, ends 0.3 m short of the facing wall or of a run on it, and is narrowed by runs on the side walls; `Walkers` takes that frame (`yaw`): the loop, the counter stop and the fridge trip are planned in it and turned back into the world, and the routing keeps people one body radius from every footprint. A room with no usable strip (the office scan) gets the whole floor and no counter stop, and the log says so. `make_tasks` judges which counter holds an object, which sink is in reach and whether a counter continues another in the counter's own frame, and the start offsets run along the counter. The scripted expert's fixed grasp yaws (fingers across the counter, hand along it for the drawer) turn with the run (`SimEnv.run_yaw`); without that the west-run `put_in_drawer` never lifted the mug. On the hero kitchen (north wall, yaw 0) nothing changes: 14 episodes over seven families at seed 5 with two people give the same 14 success lines, to the digit, before and after. `tests/fixtures/scans/rotated_kitchen` has runs on the west (facing +x), south (facing -y) and east (facing -x) walls with a sink, a fridge and a stove: 13/14 at seeds 5 and 7 and 12/14 at seed 8 with two people, 14/14 at seed 6 without. The misses are not the wall: one bowl pick lands 119° from the hand's heading, just under the expert's 120° flip threshold, and the wrist runs to its limit (the closer equivalent grasp lifts it); two `put_in_drawer` draws on the east run have the mug 0.575 m from the drawer, and the same distance on the hero kitchen's `counter_a` fails 0/3, so MAN-5's 0.6 m reach filter is loose. Still world-x in `scene.py`: `drawer_x` compares appliance x with the counter's x, so the west run's drawer lands in the middle of the counter instead of its left quarter, and `_sinks_in` tests the sink against axis-aligned counter bounds. A RoomPlan `storage` cabinet (`hinge_cabinet`) and a sink with no counter under it get no MuJoCo body, so #42's real scan would build its dishwasher, oven and fridge and the counter synthesized around its sink, and nothing for its four storage cabinets.

**Scanned kitchens get tasks, and every scanned fixture is solid.** #49 builds a floor-standing, counter-height RoomPlan `storage` cabinet through `counter_xml` and cuts a sink that sits on it into the cabinet. That gave a scanned kitchen its cabinets, but nothing used them: `make_tasks`, `propose`, `place_default_objects` and the sink success check still looked only for fixtures of type `counter`. `tests/fixtures/scans/u_kitchen_42` is laid out like the real U-shaped kitchen in #42 (a sink on a storage cabinet and a dishwasher on the east wall, an oven between two storage cabinets on the north wall, a fridge). With #75 and #49 it builds every fixture but gets 0 tasks: every family logs "no reachable variation". All four now use #49's `is_counter_host`, so a counter-height cabinet is a counter for tasks, objects and the sink's top height. Three more gaps are closed.
